Cael sat and looked at Brom across the trestle. It was not a legal question. It was not an objection anybody would ever stand up and make in those words. It was the fear that would be sitting in all three chairs on Monday whether anybody said it or not, and Brom had found it and put it on the table in front of him. Cael understood, sitting there, that if he did not answer it tonight he would answer it badly on Monday, in front of the only people whose answer mattered.

He took his time. The rehearsal was for exactly this.

"Because I'm not asking him to make it," he said slowly.

He felt along it as he said it, the way he felt along a stance for the place where the weight was carried.

"The hole's there already. It's been there for generations. Everything that's ever been done to people like me has been done in it: the flag, the courier, the file, the four in the digests who never got a hearing. All of it was done with no rule to say how, and no limit to say how far, and no appeal, because you can't appeal under a rule nobody wrote." He kept his eyes on Brom's. "Right now the hole is wearing the law's coat. That's the dangerous part. Not that it's there. That everybody pretends it isn't, and goes on doing things in it as if there were a rule."

Brom did not move.

"If the panel finds the schedule doesn't reach me," said Cael, "that isn't making a hole. It's finding one. In public, on the record, where somebody who's allowed to write law can see it." He stopped, and found the last part, and said it plainly. "A hole in the law has one remedy, and it's the same as it's always been. Somebody writes the law. Nobody ever mended a hole by pretending to stand on it."

The fire ticked.

Brom held Coss's stillness for a breath longer. Then he let it go, all at once, and was Brom again, broad and tired and flushed along the jaw. He unfolded his hands and put them flat on the trestle.

"That's the one," he said. "That answer. If anybody in that room asks why they ought to sign their names to it, that's what you tell them." He looked down at his own hands. "I didn't like doing that."

"You did it very well," said Karis, behind her bench of books. She was not in Yorlan's voice. She was not in any voice Cael had heard her use. "It was unnerving. I'd like you never to do it again."

"I'll have to do it again tomorrow."

"I know. I'd still like you never to."

Lira had come over from the fire. She stood behind Brom and put her hand on his shoulder, briefly, the way she put a hand on Cael's shoulder after a bad bout, and took it away again.

"Where did you get the leaning?" she said.

"I don't know." Brom considered it. "I watched him at the sixth sitting, by the wall, with his pen. He never leaned once. But I could see where he would, if he ever needed to. Where his weight would go." He shrugged. "It's only weight. Everybody's got to put it somewhere."

Karis had picked up her card again and was writing on it. She wrote for some time.

"Again," she said at last, in Yorlan's flat voice. "From the concession. The panel will hear it all again."

They heard it all again. They heard it twice more, until the fire was down to red, and on the last time through Brom leaned forward at exactly the same place and said *a hole in the world* in exactly the same patient voice, and Cael's stomach did not go tight at all.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-03-no-path-given/manuscript/chapter-53.md

# Chapter 53 — Eve

Brom tested him at breakfast, before he was properly awake, which was the whole point.

Cael had come down to the stable on the Sunday morning with his eyes still half shut and his coat buttoned wrong, because the three of them had kept him at Karis's bench of books until long after the fire was out. He sat down at the end of the scarred table and reached for his bowl without looking at it. Brom, across from him, waited until the spoon was halfway to his mouth.

"Custom," said Brom, in Coss's patient voice.

"Is not code," said Cael, into the barley.

He heard himself say it. He looked up. Lira, beside him, had his bread in one hand and had stopped tearing it, and Karis, at the fourth bowl, had her cup lifted and was not drinking from it.

"Again," said Karis.

"Custom," said Brom.

"Is not code." He put the spoon down. "If it were, there'd be no need for a schedule at all. The Compact could point at whatever it's always done and call that the law, and save itself the ink. It doesn't. It writes things down. It's written things into the schedule forty-one times, every time doing something wasn't enough and it needed the words to say so." He was wide awake now, and found that he had not needed to be. "Forty-one amendments. Forty-one times it chose the text over its own habits. Not once did it write my word in. And there's a chair on that panel whose whole work is telling the written code apart from the one people carry round in their heads. If the panel rules on what's always been done, it isn't ruling on the law. It's telling the room it couldn't find any."

Nobody said anything for a moment.

Karis put her cup down without having drunk from it.

"That's it," she said. "That's the whole of it. You said it with barley on your chin."

He wiped his chin.

They had found the weak place together, the four of them, on the Saturday night after Brom's hole in the world. It was the one place where the whole argument could actually break. If a magistrate wanted to, he could rule that generations of treating the flag as a classification had made it one, whether anybody had written it down or not. Karis had put it to him from behind her bench of books in Yorlan's flat voice, and he had answered it badly, and then less badly, and then well. Then she had made him answer it again, and again, while Lira timed him by the stable clock and Brom sat with his hands folded on the wood like a man who had never once in his life been in a hurry. At some point after the fire went out Karis had said that she would stop when he could say it asleep, and Lira had said that could be arranged.

"So it's in," said Brom, picking up his own spoon. "Past where you have to look for it."

"Past where I can find it to lose it," said Cael. "I hope."

"The panel notes it," said Karis, in Yorlan's voice, and then, in her own, "Eat. We've one more pass and then I'm not doing any more. I promised myself."

They did the last pass after breakfast, at the trestle by the fire, with the stable empty and the second-years gone up the hill to the town for the Sunday market. Karis sat behind her bench of books. Brom sat at the end in Coss's stillness. Lira sat on the bench with her boots up and the stable clock in her eye. Cael stood.

He gave them all of it, from the beginning. The concession first, short and flat and with nothing on it. Then the definitions clause, cited, pointed to, with *shall mean* let sit in the room a moment by itself. The schedule went through all six places, each one cleared, his word in none. Brom leaned forward at the place where he always leaned and said *a hole in the world*, and Cael told him that nobody ever mended a hole by pretending to stand on it. Karis put the custom objection in the flat voice, and he answered it with forty-one amendments and the chair whose work was the difference. And he finished where he meant to finish on Monday, with the question he meant to leave in the room.

Nobody interrupted him once.

When he stopped, nobody said anything. The fire ticked. Somewhere above them in the residence wing a door shut. He stood with his hands at his sides and found nothing in it that wanted fixing. There was not a joint in it that was not carrying weight, and all four of them could hear that. Hearing it was a strange, light feeling, like setting down a sack he had carried so long that he had stopped knowing it was on his back.

"Well," said Lira at last.

"Well," said Brom.

Karis took the top book off her bench, and then the next, and began to put them back into two piles, squaring each one against the last.

"I'm not doing any more," she said, without looking up. "It's finished. Doing it again will only wear the edges off." She squared the last book. "If you can say that in the assembly room tomorrow without your voice going, the way you've just said it to three people and a fire, then there's nothing more I can give it."

"And if my voice goes?"

"Then it goes," said Karis. "And you'll say it anyway, because it's in past where your voice is." She picked up the first pile. "That's what the drilling was for. Not so you'd be good. So you'd be the same whether you were good or not."

---

Brom found him at the middle of the day, at the rail of the defensive floor, where Cael had gone because it was Sunday and empty and he wanted to stand somewhere that smelled of chalk.

He came along the rail without hurrying and stopped beside him and put his forearms on it, as Hobb did, and for a while they both looked at the empty floor, at the chalk ghosts of Wray's marks and the scorched place on the boards where the last of Karis's channel had gone out in the gauntlet. Neither of them said anything. Between the two of them that had long been a conversation in itself, and they had it for some time.

Then Brom took a folded page out of the inside of his coat and held it out.

"Here," he said.

Cael took it and unfolded it.

It was a single sheet, in Brom's hand, the careful square capitals of his number columns gone small and joined up into ordinary writing, which Cael had seen him do only once or twice, for letters. There was a date at the top. It was not this week's. It was from Ardenmere, the night after their bout.

Under the date was the third exchange.

Brom had written it down the way he wrote down a fetch drill, every count in its place. He gave where each of them had stood when Vell called it. He wrote that Cael had come in with no speed left, because the second exchange had taken his legs, and with nothing left to throw, and how he had fought small, short angles, weight kept over his feet. He wrote what his own read had been doing: tracking, as it always tracked, the pressure of a body in front of him, the way a hand in the dark knows where a wall is. And then he wrote the place where the read had stopped tracking. *For less than a second there was nobody in front of me. I don't mean he was quick. I mean my read went to where he was and found the floor.* He wrote that he had not known what to do, and that he had done nothing, which was wrong, and that he had won the exchange anyway on feel, and that it had taken him three times as long as either of the first two. He wrote his own errors in it with exactly the same flat care as the rest. He wrote the fourth exchange in a single line: *Then I put him down, because his legs were gone and I knew it, and that's how I won the bout.* Under that, in the square capitals again, he had copied Vell's line from the ledger as she said it over them on the floor: *ATYPICAL MOVEMENT, THIRD EXCHANGE.*

Then, at the foot, a single line on its own, and his name.

*In case you ever need a witness to what you are, from someone who was trying his best to beat it.*

*Brom.*

Cael read it twice, and then he could not read it a third time for a while.

"You wrote this at the Ironyard," he said, when he could.

"The night after. In the boarding house." Brom did not look at him. He looked at the floor. "I'd never had a fight I couldn't account for. I sat up and wrote it down so I'd know I hadn't dreamed it. I was going to show it to you. Then the next day we sat on the floor and you told me about the window that wasn't a window, and we were friends by supper, and I never needed to." He turned his hands over on the rail. "I've had it in the back of my coat ever since. I've moved it to every coat. I never knew what it was for."

"It isn't evidence. The panel won't take it."

"I know. It isn't for the panel." Brom looked at him then, steadily, the way he looked at a weight he was about to lift and knew he could. "It's for you. Tomorrow, if it goes wrong, you'll start wondering whether any of it was real. You do that. I've watched you do it after every bad bout since Ardenmere. You go back over everything and decide you made it up." He nodded at the page. "So it's written. By a man who stood on that floor and had no reason to lie about it. I won that bout. Saying what happened in the third exchange doesn't make me look one bit better. It's only true."

Cael folded the page along its old creases. He did not put it in the binder. He did not put it in the observation notebook, or anywhere it would be filed among things that proved things. He put it in the inside pocket of his own coat, where it lay flat against his chest, and he could feel its edge there when he breathed.

"Thank you," he said.

"Don't." Brom straightened up off the rail. "Whatever they rule tomorrow, that page says the same thing it said the night I wrote it." He began to walk away along the rail, and then stopped, and turned half round. "And if they rule your way, you can give it back. I'd like it back. It's the best thing I ever wrote."

---

He took the last of the borrowed books down to the archive in the middle of the afternoon.

There were nine of them, Prynn's volumes that had lived on the long table and at his elbow for three weeks: the enforcement digests, the provision's history in its boxes, the charter's three volumes, the schedule's index. He carried them down the archive passage in two armfuls, and the second armful was heavier than the first, because the charter was in it.

The front of the founding stock was bare. He did not look at it for long. The back was bare too, now, all nine cases, the dust standing in pale oblongs on every shelf, as far back as the old grey wall. He had known it would be. Knowing did not stop it from catching in his throat.

Prynn was on her high stool with the marbled book shut beside her and her own ledger open. She watched him come in with the first armful and set it down on the end of her desk, and go back, and come in with the second and set that down too. Then she got off the stool and went through the nine volumes one at a time, checking each spine against the slip she had kept in its place on the shelf. She held each slip up to the spine, and read them both, and said *hm*, and laid the slip on the desk. She did it slowly. He had never once seen her do anything to a book quickly.

At the ninth she put the slip down with the others and squared the pile of slips against the edge of the desk.

"Every one," she said. "In the same state it went out. Two of them in better." She looked at him over her spectacles. "You read the provision's boxes in their order and put them back in their order. Nobody does that."

"You told me to."

"I tell everybody to." She laid her hand on the top volume of the pile, the charter's first. Then, instead of picking it up, she took her hand off it, and turned back to her stool. "You know where they live now. Put them back yourself."

He stood still.

In sixty years, he thought, she had very likely never said that to anybody. Nobody put anything back on Prynn's shelves but Prynn. Readers took books down with a slip in the gap, and Prynn put them back, and that was the archive and always had been. He picked up the charter's first volume, and then the second and third, and carried them down the long room between the cases to the very back. Behind the pillar, by the old wall, the case stood floor to ceiling with its fascicles hanging their faded tapes like roots. He found the three gaps where the slips had been and slid each volume home, spine exactly in line with its neighbours, and stood back and looked at them.

When he came back for the next armful Prynn was writing in her ledger, and did not look up. He thought that her mouth had moved at one corner, and he could not have sworn to it.

He did the rest, the digests to the fourth shelf of the second case, the provision's history to its boxes. When he had finished Karis was at the long table.

She had come in while he was at the back, and she was sitting at their end of it, the far end, where they had sat across from each other since the week she first asked his leave. She had her notebooks in front of her. Not the satchel, not the long flat box of cards, which was empty now and had nothing left to index. She had the notebooks themselves, the whole of the second one, all its volumes, in a stack, oldest at the bottom and newest at the top, squared at the corners.

He came and sat down across from her.

"I've something for you," said Karis.

She took the top volume off the stack, the newest, the one that had been buttoned inside her coat on Thursday night, and opened it at the back. She did not open it at the copies of the register. She opened it at the very last written page, and turned it round, and slid it across the table to him.

It was nearly blank. There was a date at the top, today's, in her small closed hand. Under the date there was a heading she had underlined once. And under the heading there were two sentences, and nothing else on the page at all.

*Findings to date: the phenomenon is a person. Study continues.*

He read it twice.

"That's all?" he said. "Five years of notebooks, and that's the finding?"

"That's the one that's true." She watched him read it a third time. "I came over that hill to study something nobody could explain. I had a stack of notebooks full of wrong explanations and a column of things I'd killed, and I wanted to stand next to the thing when it finally made sense." She put her hand flat on the stack. "And somewhere in the last three weeks I noticed what I'd been writing down. That he reads every letter from Denvash twice before he answers it. That he gives back the larger half of the bread when he thinks nobody's counting. That a man asked him on the record whether he knew the form was wrong, and he said *No* and then nothing, and walked out of the room lighter. That a boy who beat him once wrote down the one exchange he couldn't beat." She glanced at Cael's coat, at the place over his chest, and he understood that Brom had told her, or that she had seen Brom go to the rail with his hand on his coat. "A phenomenon doesn't do any of those things. A person does."

"So you've stopped."

"I've done nothing of the kind." She said it plainly. "I've changed what I'm studying. That's not the same as stopping. It's the opposite of stopping, if anything." She took the volume back, gently, and closed it, and laid it on top of the stack, square. "I wrote it twice. One page is in that book, and the other is in your copy pile, because clause six says you read it first and keep it. I put it in the back of your binder this morning while you were at breakfast being told about custom. I didn't read anything else. I didn't look."

"I know you didn't."

"I wanted you to hear me say it." She looked at the stack of notebooks. "Study continues. I've no intention of stopping. I'd like that understood, so nobody's surprised in a year."

He did not know what to say. He sat at the table across from her, and the archive was very quiet round them, with its bare back shelves and its empty box of cards. He thought that this was as near as she would ever come to saying the thing in the way Lira or Brom would have said it, and that it was more than enough. It was exactly her size.

"Noted," he said.

Karis looked at him for a moment. Then she laughed, short, and put her hand over her mouth, and looked at Prynn at the high desk.

Prynn did not look up from her ledger. But she said, to the page, in her dry exact voice, "The archive notes it too," and turned the page over.

---

The yard was empty when he came up from the archive, and nobody had drawn a ring.

He stood at the corner of the stable in the grey end of the afternoon and looked at the place by the pump where the chalk had been every evening for three weeks. Somebody had swept it. The frozen earth showed the broom's strokes, and nothing else. For a moment he did not know what to do with his hands.

Then he saw Lira, up on the wall by the residence wing door, sitting with her knees drawn up and her coat round them, where she sat in the evenings when there was nothing to do. She was not looking at him. She was looking at the town.

He went and sat beside her.

"No drill," she said.

"No?"

"Not tonight. You've been drilled. Karis has drilled you, and Brom has drilled you, and you've drilled yourself in your sleep, and I've drilled you on that ring every dusk since the Warden came up the hill." She did not turn her head. "If I drill you tonight you'll only be thinking about the drill."

They sat. Below them the town was going blue, and the first lamps were coming on along the good street. The draper's back window was dark yet.

Lira put her hand inside her coat and took out the envelope with the green wax on it. He knew it. She had carried it there since the ninth week, against her chest, with the four paragraphs inside that a man at Fenmark had signed without ever meeting her. She did not open it. She slid a finger under the flap and drew out, from behind the folded sheet, a smaller slip of paper, folded once. It was soft at the edges, as if it had been taken out and put back a good many times.

She held it out to him.

He unfolded it. It was her quick slanting scrawl, in pencil, already rubbed a little grey.

*The records know what happened here. Nobody gets to say otherwise.*

"You said it on this wall," said Lira. "The night I'd read the four paragraphs and decided they didn't get to tell me who I was. You'd had it off Vell, and you turned it round and gave it to me. I wrote it down that night so I'd have it in your words and not just mine." She looked at the slip in his hand. "I've had it behind Fenmark ever since. Every time I took the four paragraphs out to read them again, which I did more than I'd ever tell you, that came out first."

"Lira."

"I don't need it tomorrow. You do." She put the envelope back in her coat and buttoned the coat over it. "Tomorrow the Compact writes a record with one of us standing up in the middle of it, talking. Not a line about us, written in a room we weren't in. One of us, talking. So you keep that in your pocket. And you make the record say something worth keeping."

He folded the slip and put it in the inside pocket of his coat, against Brom's page. The two of them lay there flat together, and he could feel both edges when he breathed.

Lira slid down off the wall.

"I lied," she said. "One more thing."

She walked out into the middle of the swept yard, where there was no ring, and turned round, and set her feet. It was not a Greyvane stance. It was the one from the yard behind the grain exchange in Ardenmere, weight a little forward and hands low, the first thing she had ever shown him on purpose. He had been a boy who wrote fights down in a notebook then, and she had been a girl with a fist of stolen bread and no school.

He went out and matched it.

They did not fight, exactly. There was nobody to throw anything and nobody to count, and the frozen ruts were uneven under his boots where the broom had not reached. They went through the oldest patterns there were, the first-year forms, his evasion framework against her Wind at the speed of somebody walking home. Then a little faster. Then, without either of them saying so, at the speed they had used on the circuit, when there had been a ledger and a crowd and a purse. His feet found the ruts and stopped minding them. On the fourth turn the pivot that had been a hair late all week came exactly on time, and he felt it come, and she saw him feel it.

"There," said Lira, and did not stop.

She came in again, and he turned her, and she went past him and round and came in from the other side.

"Tomorrow they'll spend the whole morning telling you what you are," she said, between one exchange and the next. Her breath was going up white and quick. "In their words. Coss will. He'll stand there for an hour and read you out of his case, and every word will be so, and none of it will be you." She turned out of his reach. "Let him. When it's your turn, don't start from his words. Don't start from theirs at all."

"Where, then?"

She stopped. She stood in front of him in the near dark with her hands down, breathing hard, and put two fingers flat on his breastbone, gently, the scoring touch from the circuit, and left them there.

"Here," she said. "This is real. Start from that."

He stood with her two fingers on his chest and the cold coming up through his boots, and understood what she meant, and that she was not talking about the argument at all. The argument was finished. She was talking about whatever stood on the floor underneath it.

She took her hand away.

"That's all," said Lira. "That's the whole drill." She wiped her face on her sleeve. "I'll be in the gallery tomorrow, on the bench behind Oona, by the aisle. I'm not allowed to hit anybody. Quenna told me twice, and Gerda told me the clerk can put me out for a cough." She considered it. "If they rule against you, I'll still not hit anybody. I'd like that noted somewhere."

"Noted," said Cael.

Lira laughed, short and surprised, and then she reached out and knocked her shoulder against his, the old knock, and went in.
---

He was alone when he got the binder out, at the desk by the narrow window, with the lamp turned low and nothing in the window but his own face against the dark yard.

Brom's page and Lira's slip were in the coat on the chair, and he knew where they were without touching them. Karis's other copy was in the back pocket of the binder, behind her consent and her paragraph and the page she had cut out of her ledger on the Tuesday. The observation notebook lay shut on the corner of the desk with two lines in a box near the front of it, not for tomorrow. His outline was in his head, past where he would have to look for it.

Tomorrow a magistrate of the Compact would sit in the assembly room in the lecture wing with two hundred people on the benches and the doors open at the back. Coss would stand up and lay out a case that was clean and fair and would hold, and every word of it would be true. And then Cael would stand up, in front of all of them, and tell a room full of people with the authority to end his place here that their authority had never once reached him.

He opened the binder at a clean page and wrote in the hand he kept for the entries that had to last longer than the mood they were written in.

*Tomorrow I argue that their system never contained me.*

He sat with it a while. Then, because there was one more true thing, and the binder was for true things, he went on.

*I thought I'd be angry, by now. I'm not. Everything I've read for three weeks says the same thing underneath. The flag, the courier who walked me out of Denvash, the file that followed me here: not one of them ever had a line of the code under it that held me. They did all of it anyway. That's the part I'd have wanted fourteen to hear. Tomorrow I'll say it to the Compact instead.*

He read it over. He did not cross anything out.

Under it, smaller, he added the rest of the day, because the binder was for what had happened as well as for what he thought.

*K.: findings to date, the phenomenon is a person. Study continues. B.: a page from the Ironyard, the third exchange, which is in my coat and not in here. L.: this is real, start from that. And she gave me back the records line. P. let me shelve the code myself.*

He closed the binder, and turned the lamp down until it was nearly out, and sat a moment longer in front of the dark window.

Down the hill the town's lamps were going out one by one, as they did every night. The last one burning was in an upper window at the back of the draper's shop, a small square of steady yellow, where a man read every case from the first page and would be reading this one now, again, from the beginning. Cael watched it. He did not hate it. He found he could not.

He put the lamp out and lay down in the dark.

It did not come quickly. The fear came first, as it always came on the last night before a floor, and it was the size it always was. He knew it well enough by now to let it lie down beside him without arguing. Under it, for the first time he could remember, there was something that did not move. Two folded papers lay flat against each other in the coat on the chair. A notebook in the archive had a finding in it with today's date. Nine volumes stood on their shelves where he had put them himself, and the pivot had come on time in a swept yard at dusk.

In the morning the assembly room would be full. The benches would be full and the doors would be open at the back, and people would stand in the passage to hear. Most of them had come up the hill, or across the yard, or out of the residence wing in their good coats, to watch a boy from nowhere lose to the most careful man in the Compact's service.

He thought about them for a while in the dark, and then he stopped, because there was nothing left to fix.

He slept.

---

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.


---

## NUMERICAL REVIEW

# Numerical formula alignment — after the movement

This is an informed editorial pass, not a blind cold read. Read the supplied
formula and full movement, then report target, observed result, method, coverage
and uncertainty. Keep numerical alignment separate from reader scores.

- Sentence mean, median, population standard deviation; shares ≤5 and ≥40 words.
- Written paragraph mean/median and actual scene/section boundaries. Compare with
  the requested targets, but do not equate typography with ASR pause segmentation.
- Flesch Reading Ease and Flesch-Kincaid grade. Identify the sentence tokenizer and
  syllable estimator; proper names/system terms can affect estimates.
- Progression vocabulary hits per 10,000 words, and placement by equal word-count
  book thirds once available. Define a project-specific lexicon before counting;
  do not borrow the source novel's names. An incomplete book cannot confirm the
  full front-loading ratio. Report provisional cumulative coverage instead.
- Lead development moments and supporting-cast distribution: cite actual choices
  or realizations and distinguish manually identified beats from regex proxies.
- POV word share: identify viewpoint segments, not the most frequently mentioned
  name. Track the lead and secondary viewpoints against the book-level allocation.
- Secondary signals: -ly words, reporting verbs, combat terms, fillers and repeated
  phrases. Publish lexicons and counting definitions. A suffix count is not proof
  that every matched word is an adverb. Preserve optional/non-target distinctions.

Use reproducible counts when tooling exists. Otherwise mark a metric UNMEASURED;
never estimate a precise score by intuition. Save the method or command with the
report so later runs use the same definitions. Inspect ASR reference limitations
without discarding the owner's chosen drafting targets.

Locate meaningful departures, especially uniform sentence lengths, missing short
beats, reading level drift, dense paragraphs, late teaching or a coequal second
lead replacing the requested POV shape. Consolidate the most important repairs
with continuity findings into the existing bounded author repair. Protect exact
owner text and successful scenes. Do not iterate indefinitely to hit decimals or
hide irreconcilable targets. Bring consequential formula conflicts to the owner.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — editions/monroe-1.3/EDITION_BRIEF.md

# The Fractured Path — Monroe Jackson 1.3 edition (O'Connor 1.3 seat)

Owner direction, 2026-10-01: rewrite the whole Fractured Path series through the
O'Connor 1.3 seat on the Monroe Jackson 1.3 foundation, with Claude Opus 5.5 as the
manuscript author, at about 300,000 words per book. When a book is complete, render
every chapter through Breeze and publish it as a new edition. The current edition
stays exactly as it is; this edition is new and recoverable.

Public byline: Monroe Jackson. Seat: `oconnor` 1.3.0. Foundation: Monroe Jackson 1.3.0.
Requested author: Opus (Claude Opus 5.5). Coordinator: this orchestrator session.
Review seat: Sol (gpt-5.6-sol via `codex exec`) — a different model family from the author.

## What "rewrite" means here

This is a retelling at full length, not a copy-edit. The prose is new. The story is the
same series.

**Keep (canon — binding):**
- Every book's spine: the major events, their order and outcomes, who lives and dies,
  the ending state, ages, the calendar where later books depend on it.
- Cael's fragment acquisitions — which fragment, in which book, under what engagement —
  and the counts at each book's close. The system notice texts that later books quote.
- The SECRET reveal schedule in `universe/CANON_RULES.md` and every reserved truth. A
  rewrite may plant more; it may never disclose earlier than the schedule.
- The universe rules, costs and limits in `universe/UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`.
- Relationships and their turning points as the existing books establish them.
- Lines a later book quotes or calls back (log entries, notices, signature exchanges).
  The book planner lists these as protected wording.

**Change (the point of the edition):**
- Length: about 300,000 words per book (owner). The existing books are 100–125k. The
  extra ~190k comes from: developed fights given room (1,500–2,500+ words when the
  changing problem earns it); learning encounters and training that show effort turning
  into capability; events the current edition summarizes, put on the page; supporting
  cast given private wants, independent decisions and arcs across the formula's 8–10
  person cast; humor and warmth between people; brief POV cutaways; new episodes that
  fit canon and do not alter any book's end state.
- Shape: open-range movements of six or more chapters, one compact brief per movement,
  no per-chapter cards or per-chapter gates. Roughly 55–65 chapters of ~5,000 words per
  book in 7–9 movements; the author owns chapter boundaries.
- Rhythm: the owner-selected numerical formula (compiled into every prompt). Baseline
  of the current edition, measured by `tools/formula_metrics.py`: sentence mean ~17.5
  (target 14.6), paragraph median ~44 words (target ~18), Flesch RE ~67 (target 72.3),
  FK grade ~8.3 (target 6.8). The edition should move toward the targets.
- POV: about 87% Cael, about 13% brief purposeful cutaways spread over 4–5 named
  characters. Cutaways may never reveal what a reserved-truth boundary withholds.

## Non-negotiable

- **Reader Standard (Gate 27, owner):** written for a thirteen-year-old. Clean language
  (no profanity, obscenity, crude slang or blasphemy, including "damn", "hell" as an
  oath, "bastard" and softer cousins); moral goodness (honesty, courage, loyalty,
  restraint, care for the weak, at a cost; wrong named as wrong; cruelty never
  rewarded); violence with cost, fear and consequence and no gore; no sexual content
  or innuendo. Outranks every voice preference. See `craft/VOICE_CHARTER.md` end section.
- **Names:** keep every existing canon name. The registry's flagged collisions
  (Vell/Velmere, the -vane cluster, Wray/Greyvane, Bracken/Brom) are the owner's call
  and are NOT renamed in this edition. New minor characters the expansion needs may be
  named by the planner, screened by ear against `craft/NAME_REGISTRY.md`, and listed
  under "Names pending owner approval" in the book map. Never invent a name for an
  existing canon role.
- **Audio-first:** this ships as an audiobook. Clear referents, clear attribution,
  names distinct by ear, punctuation that exposes meaning.

## Where things live

- Canon (read-only for this edition): `universe/UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`,
  `universe/CANON_RULES.md`, `universe/STATE_LEDGER.md`, `craft/NAME_REGISTRY.md`,
  `craft/VOICE_CHARTER.md`, `series/THE_FRACTURED_PATH_SERIES.md`.
- Source edition (the story being retold): `books/<book>/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md` and
  `books/<book>/chapters/chapter-NN.md`. Read them for events, not for sentences.
- This edition: `editions/monroe-1.3/<book>/` — `BOOK_MAP.md`, `STATE_LEDGER.md`,
  `packets/MOVEMENT-NNN.md`, `manuscript/chapter-NN.md`, `state/movement-NNN/`.
- Series-level edition maps: `editions/monroe-1.3/SERIES_MAP.md`, `CHARACTERS.md`.
- Metrics: `python3 editions/monroe-1.3/tools/formula_metrics.py <chapters...>`.

## Rhythm calibration (measured, 2026-10-01)

Book 1 Movement 1 — the first movement drafted from this brief — overshot the formula in
the short direction: sentence mean 8.8 words (target 14.6), 41% of sentences at five
words or fewer (target ~28%), 0.3% at forty-plus (target ~3.3%), Flesch-Kincaid grade 2.8
(target 6.8), about 106 dialogue tags per 10k words (target ~41). Paragraph median (15)
and scene spacing were close. So, when drafting:

- Let thought, action and description run in full, well-built sentences. Compound and
  complex sentences with a clear hierarchy are the house texture, not the exception.
- Join a run of three or four clipped statements into one sentence when they are one thought.
- Keep short sentences for beats that land — a recognition, a hit, a turn — not as default.
- Give each chapter a few deliberately long, readable sentences (forty words or more)
  where an action or a thought earns the length.
- Drop "he said" / "she said" when the paragraph already makes the speaker clear.
- Keep short paragraphs (median ~18 words) and scene breaks about every 950 words.

## No scripted prose surgery (2026-10-01)

Rhythm and length repairs are done by reading, sentence by sentence, never by a script that
splits sentences or paragraphs at clause or sentence boundaries. Two repairs did that and
each needed a recheck to find the splits that broke a thought. Where the formula and a
character's speech disagree, keep the speech: one-to-three-word dialogue lines and the short
landing beats of a fight are not drift to be repaired.

## Working ranges and accepted drift (coordinator, 2026-10-01, after five repairs)

Five movement repairs show three of the formula's numbers pulling against the brief's other
asks (full sentences as house texture, speech kept as people speak, paragraphs broken where the
thought turns). Repairs that chase one of them push another out. So, until the owner rules
otherwise, these are the working ranges a movement is held to, and repairs aim at them — not at
the decimals:

| Measure | Formula target | Working range | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sentence mean | 14.6 | 13–15.5 | primary target — keep |
| ≥40-word share | 3.3% | 2.5–4.5% | primary target — keep |
| Words per scene | ~950 | 850–1,050 | primary target — keep |
| ≤5-word share | 27.7% | up to ~34% | dialogue-heavy movements; short speech is kept |
| Paragraph median | ~18 | up to ~30 | the 18 is an ASR pause proxy, not print; long cutaways run longer |
| Flesch-Kincaid grade | 6.8 | 3.5–6 | clear short words; the syllable estimator is approximate |

Measured drift outside a working range is reported in the movement's review; a repair is for
the primary three and for concrete defects.

## Source-reuse gate tightened to 8 words (coordinator, 2026-10-02)

The 10-word gate let distinctive source phrasing through in 6–9-word fragments (the first
movement's opening line, for example, echoed the current edition's). Measured at 8 words, movements
written after authors began self-checking run 1–6 shared runs per 10k; the worst ran ~30. From now
on `ed.sh overlap` and every editorial review use runs of **8 or more** words. Every listed run that
BOOK_MAP does not protect is rewritten — re-composed, not a synonym swapped. Names, Path names,
ranks and plain facts of canon will sometimes share a short run; re-compose the sentence around them.
Movements closed under the 10-word gate get one book-level 8-word sweep at book completion.

## Draft from your own event list, not from the source page (coordinator, 2026-10-02)

Book 3 Movement 6's review found the retold scenes in ch42–44 to be close paraphrase: 23–32% of
sentences followed a source sentence word for word in order, with words varied enough to pass the
8-word gate. Everything the author invented was clean. The cause is drafting with the source open.
So, for every movement:

1. Read the source chapters the packet names, once, for what happens and who these people are.
2. Close them. Write a private event list in your own words — what happens, in what order, who
   wants what, what changes. Keep it in your working notes, not in the manuscript.
3. Draft from that list and the book map. Do not reopen the source chapters while drafting.
   Choose your own entry point into each scene and your own order of beats; the source's scene
   shape is not canon, only its events are.
4. Only protected wording (BOOK_MAP) is copied, exactly, from the map — not from the source page.

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — editions/monroe-1.3/book-03-no-path-given/BOOK_MAP.md

# BOOK MAP — The Fractured Path, Book 3: No Path Given
## Monroe Jackson 1.3 edition (O'Connor seat 1.3.0)

- Public byline: Monroe Jackson. Seat `oconnor` 1.3.0. Foundation Monroe Jackson 1.3.0.
- Requested manuscript author: Opus (Claude Opus 5.5). Planning seat runtime model (self-reported): claude-opus-5-5.
- Status: PLAN ONLY. No manuscript prose exists for this edition. Nothing here is evidence that chapters were written.
- Target: ~300,000 words, 61 chapters, 8 movements (all six chapters or more; none over ~42,000 words).
- Read for this plan, in full: `editions/monroe-1.3/EDITION_BRIEF.md`, `universe/CANON_RULES.md`, `universe/UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`, `universe/STATE_LEDGER.md`, `series/THE_FRACTURED_PATH_SERIES.md` (Books 2–4 and the spoiler ledger), `craft/NAME_REGISTRY.md`, `books/book-03-no-path-given/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md`, source chapters 1–24 in order, Book 2 source chapters 23–24, Book 4 source chapter 1. Later-book callbacks were checked by search across Books 4–8 (results folded into Protected Wording and Canon Conflicts below).
- Owner names the cast. Every existing canon name is kept, including the registry's flagged collision names (Wray is NOT renamed in this edition). Three new minor names are proposed below and are pending approval; until approved the author uses the role description.

---

## 0. NOW / NEXT / HORIZON

- **NOW:** Movement 1 (chapters 1–8). Arrival at Greyvane, the three tracks, the observer cage turned vantage point, the first demonstration, Edran's public question. The progression system is taught hard here. Packet: `packets/MOVEMENT-001.md`.
- **NEXT:** Movements 2–3. Karis arrives and asks; the terms; Coss is assigned; Karis takes the standings; Edran's petition and the exhibition; Karis's hypothesis and the covenant on the wall.
- **HORIZON:** Movements 4–8. Lira's file and Brom's wall; twenty-two null sessions; the directed acquisition; Coss's challenge; three weeks of registry law; the older word; two sittings; the record made public; the hand-off to Book 4's final Greyvane term and Halcenvane.

---

## 1. Entry state (binding — from Book 2 source ch23–24, State Ledger, series bible)

- **Calendar:** No time skip. Book 3 opens on the third morning of the road east from Ardenmere, the same three travelers, same easy pace. Season by the source's weather: early autumn. Cael is **fifteen** (LOCKED) and stays fifteen for the whole book. No birthday is staged for anyone.
- **Cael:** [SHATTERED] in the Compact Registry; Iron-equivalent by Vell's circuit ledger (beat Reydan, Iron R8, at B2's climax). Four confirmed fragments — Wind-adjacent (Lira, B1), Pressure-adjacent (Feryn, B1), Iron-adjacent (Brom, B2 Ch13), Compression-adjacent (Reydan, notice B2 Ch24) — plus one unconfirmed anomaly (Tide-adjacent reading, session nine with Brom, B2 Ch19). Compression is days old and clumsy: the road sessions caught about a quarter of a slow push on the sixth or seventh try, at a toll through shoulder and sternum; the second try mis-routed into his own knee. The redirect shoulder from the Reydan bout still "announces itself on certain angles."
- **Carried objects:** the Power Log (a binder now); Hesk's leather-bound notebook; the older observation notebook (holds the copied line from Vell's pre-standardization archive: "Assessed per pre-registry terminology as UNBOUND."); Vell's hand-copied sheaf of his whole Ironyard record; the stranger's note ("For whatever it's worth: you fought honestly. That matters here."); Hesk's letter ("Go. You need people who have seen you clearly and haven't left. Find more of them."). Cael's reply promised: "I'll write when I arrive."
- **Knowledge:** Lira and Brom both know about the fragments and have read the Power Log. Lira has known since B1 Ch16. Cael knows of four recorded [SHATTERED] instances. He knows Coss and Havel have monitored him. He does NOT know the Suppression-Advisory Watch, Priority Level 4 designation exists.
- **Lira:** Wind Path, Copper formal, expelled from Fenmark at fourteen for "insubordination" two years ago; has never read the Fenmark file; going in as a re-certification candidate.
- **Brom:** Iron Skin Path, Copper formal, Iron-equivalent circuit rating; left Velmere about two years before B2 Ch8; applying for standard enrollment on what he can demonstrate.
- **Greyvane (known from B2 Ch22–23):** minor-tier academy; Quenna (Silver, irregular-Path development assessment; scout and assessor of record) holds the paperwork for three tracks: demonstration provision/observer (Cael), re-certification (Lira), standard (Brom).
- **Compact:** passive surveillance; Coss is supervising contact of record and has filed an unanswered upward note about the Level 4; Havel noticed the marker and keeps a private note (two entries). Ilsev is not on the case.
- **Open threads carried untouched:** Reydan's "Find me later — I want the answer when you have it"; Vell's offered archive session; the patient Iron Skin watcher (B2 Ch15); the B1 market stranger; Hesk's full history; Coss's exact grade.

## 2. Ending state (binding — from source ch23–24, architecture checkpoint, and Book 4 Ch1)

- **Ruling:** the challenge is dismissed for want of established jurisdiction; enrollment standing remanded to the academy; classification status "undefined under the current schedule." Public record. Naveth re-papers the enrollment the same day on the academy's internal authority.
- **Cael:** fifteen. Five confirmed fragments: Wind (Lira), Pressure (Feryn), Iron (Brom), Compression (Reydan), Ember (Karis — the only one whose notice carries "Acquisition: directed"). One anomaly, session nine, Tide-adjacent: still unreproduced, unexplained, uncounted. The Ember point appears on Greyvane's record in exactly one demonstration exhibit, recorded late in the term and thinly, and that exhibit sits in the hearing record (Book 4 Ch4, Ch21). The directed mechanism is known to Lira, Brom and Karis only — never in any record. Quenna has theories she has refused to write down. Cael has not answered Quenna's question (whether he can not do it); Book 4 answers it.
- **Karis:** in the circle. Holds two hand copies of the waystation passage; Cael holds a third in the observation notebook. Has found the shape of where a demonstration-evaluation precedent should be, and has NOT found it (Book 4 final term, week three, finds the "assay clause"). By Book 4's arrangement she reads the Power Log by asking; this book stages the asking (see §5, Karis).
- **Lira:** Copper formal; re-certification file carries "Advancement inconsistent with recorded tier. Recommend continued track with priority review." Keeps Fenmark's four paragraphs; has filed no dispute.
- **Brom:** standard enrollment, good standing; Wray has named his wall (a gap between reading an incoming force and executing the redirect; it matters only at Silver-tier speed); Greyvane has nobody fast enough to train him past it.
- **Edran:** Iron R2, second on the standings ("formerly first"); seam rebuilt; has requested a rematch next semester — "Full sanction, faculty supervised, on the record."
- **Quenna:** has told Cael the outgrowing "will be soon."
- **Prynn:** the founding-era boxes are back; the waystation registry with the crosshatched sevens did not come back although the seizure inventory's count says it did; she leaves its gap on the shelf, unentered in any ledger.
- **Coss:** lost on the record; filed an honest final report recommending "registry review, not escalation"; leaves. A faceless senior office has requested the whole transcript and cut a file cover — the schedule's file, not Cael's. Vastin is not named, described or placed.
- **Havel:** three entries in his private note. **Ilsev:** has referred the Level 4 as an administrative irregularity; no answer.
- **Compact posture:** active tracking. Open watchers at Greyvane's gate and in the town market (five by Cael's count; one works mornings only).
- **Letters:** Hesk's reply received (protected). The first letter to Vell sent, signed with his circuit rating.
- **Book 4 Ch1 hand-off facts:** the next term (about 22 weeks) is Greyvane's final term for them and happens off-page between books. Nothing in Book 3 may find the assay clause, run Edran's rematch, or move them out of Greyvane.

---

## 3. Calendar (week anchors; the author may move a scene a few days, not across an anchor)

The source's internal clock contradicts itself (see §12). This calendar reconciles the source with Book 4's hard numbers (null series begins week nine; twenty-two nulls; six weeks of sessions before the match). Week 0 is arrival. Demonstration assessments ("D") are monthly-ish.

| 5–6 | Karis vs Edran standings bout (new); Karis takes first |

| 8 | Tuesday/Thursday sessions settle; Karis's hypothesis; Cael's two days; the covenant on the wall |
| 8–9 | Lira's formal reassessment and the Fenmark file; Brom's wall |
| 9–11 | Twenty-two controlled null sessions (from week nine); the Tide anomaly told in the third week |
| 12 | **D4** (routine); Quenna proposes the match as D5; Karis's consent |
| 12–15 | Ember study; Lira's lattice drills; conditions posted; taper |
| 16 | **D5 = the match.** "Acquisition: directed." |

Season runs from early autumn to midwinter (breath showing at dawn by week 16; cold reading-room floors in the three weeks).

## 4. The spine — every major event of the source, in order (all KEEP)

Each item is binding in outcome and order. Wording is new unless listed under Protected Wording.

1. KEEP — The road's last climb; the hill town below; Greyvane smaller than "academy" promised; the gate clerk has a list with their three names on it (src 1).
2. ## 5. The expansion plan (~190,000 words added)

Nothing below changes any end state, fragment count, death, order of events, or reveal date. New material lives in the gaps the source summarizes or skips.

### 5.1 Fights and learning encounters given room

| Encounter | Movement | Allowance | Why it earns the room |
|---|---|---|---|
| Brom vs the broad defensive student under Wray's variable-isolation drill | M1 | ~1,500 | Shows Wray's method (change one condition, watch what breaks) — the same method that later finds Brom's wall |
| Lira vs the Copper standings student | M1 | ~1,800 | First proof that the floor and the file disagree; her hit taken on purpose |
| D1 as a learning set piece | M1 | ~2,000 | Teaches the provision's three words, the panel, and what banking looks like |
| Karis vs Edran standings bout (NEW) | M2 | ~2,000 | Puts the lattice on the page early (so M5 is predictable in hindsight); Edran loses first place and his vertigo becomes a motive. Karis wins at "public draft" economy — four exchanges, Edran yields to the geometry rather than test it — so nobody, including Cael, sees her wartime economy before M5 |
| The exhibition vs Edran | M3 | 2,500–3,000 | The observer's first public, logged fight; seam found by patience; the opponent learns mid-fight |
| Lira's reassessment, two bouts | M4 | ~3,000 total | Arithmetic performed in public; she spends hits like money |
| Twenty-two null sessions | M4 | ~3,500 across scenes | Failure that teaches a rule; the Iron read starts registering her points as density before light (plant for M5) |
| Lira's lattice drills (chalk circles, beanbags; 11 → 6 → 4) | M5 | ~2,000 | Effort turning into capability; the decision-speed lesson |
| THE MATCH | M5 | 6,500–7,500 incl. reset | The book's first central scene; three exchanges each changing the problem |
| Brom's speed gauntlet (NEW) | M6 | ~2,000 | Wray rotates Edran, Karis, Lira and the broad student at him in sequence to manufacture speed; he learns to see the gap and cannot close it — a loss that buys knowledge and honors canon's "nobody here is fast enough" |
| D6 under Coss's observation (NEW) | M6 | ~1,500 | Pays off Quenna's D1 line ("when someone hostile is reading the file"); Cael elects to show the Ember point at contact — the one thin exhibit Book 4 requires |
| The deposition as a duel of answers | M7 | ~2,500 | Answer the question asked, never the question implied |
| Prynn vs the delegation's records officer, clause by clause (on page; source has it secondhand) | M7 | ~1,200 | Prynn's craft and her loss on a single word |
| Lira's dusk spar (eve) | M7 | ~1,200 | "This is real. Start from that." |
| The two sittings | M8 | ~13,000 total | The argument rendered in the series' fight register: commitment, seam, timing |

### 5.2 Learning and training arcs (effort into capability)

- **Compression apprenticeship** (M1–M5): road sessions continue (quarter of a slow push, clumsy, costly) → half a push (M2) → full slow push and a first fast catch (M4) → hardened enough to survive one deliberate crossing of a heat channel in the match, at the price of a forearm burn (M5). Each step has a cost the reader can feel (shoulder and sternum toll; the knee mis-route once).
- **The Iron read refined** (M1–M5): through three grades of cloth (M3–M4 sessions), eyes closed at D1, then — in the null sessions at three paces — a faint density where her points are about to bloom. In the match it gives him her points "as pressure before they were light."
- **Wind framework under constraint** (M1, M3, M5): panel speed; anticipation scales with the opponent's body, thins against faster opponents; spent hard in the match; two misfires afterward.
- **The Ember apprenticeship begins** (M6, M8): only after three clean days. Contact only, one point, his own hand. It is not what he expects; Karis rewords what he took twice in Book 3 (Book 4 says the understanding took two months and three rewordings — the third and the "decision point" understanding belong to the off-page final term). The cost is real and invisible to watchers.
- **Registry law as a training floor** (M6–M7): reading in enactment order; amendments as confessions; scars where the code learned fear; the wrong road; the definitions clause; the schedule walked whole. Karis reads the schedule with him on evenings in the final ten days as an independent second reader (this makes Book 4 Ch22 true).
- **Brom's wall** (M4, M6): Wray teaches him to see it; the gauntlet proves seeing is not closing.
- **Edran's rebuild** (M3, M6, M8): narrows the gap in the exhibition; by week 24 his stance is visibly lower and the seam closed (sets up Book 4's rematch).

### 5.3 Summarized events put on the page

The road's last Compression sessions; Brom's coursework week; Lira's separate sign-in morning; D2 (one paragraph in the source); Karis's own arrival week and demonstrations for new instructors; the first Tuesday/Thursday sessions; the twenty-two nulls as distinct sessions with changing variables; D4; the consent sheet's drafting; the observer child's Kindling; Naveth's counsel's three days; Brom's witness interviews; Lira's maintenance drills; the four enforcement files read; Prynn's seizure contest; the boxes leaving and returning; Coss writing his report; the watcher count's first days; Naveth's correspondence; D7.

### 5.4 Supporting-cast arcs (formula: 8–10 people, many small beats, no single deuteragonist)

| Person | Private want | Independent decisions on the page | Beats (approx.) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lira | To be acknowledged on her own terms — then to stop needing it | Signs the acknowledgment; fights not holding back; reads the file; files no dispute; keeps the four paragraphs; drills him without mercy; sets the three-clean-days rule; appoints herself to keep him a fighter; helps another re-certification candidate when the floor does not rescue her; releases Karis from her promise not to ask about the log | 18 |
| Karis | To be standing next to the open question when it resolves — and to learn that belonging is not a ranking | Transfers; waits a week as an ordinary student; asks permission; brings it to the table; hands over her notebooks and shares one declaration; takes first place from Edran; volunteers as source and writes her own consent; asks to be inside; chooses the truth search over the argument; copies the passage twice; accepts Cael's refusal; starts the demonstration-precedent search; asks to read the log | 18 |
| Brom | A teacher who fixes instead of uses; an honest ceiling; to be a true witness | Chooses Wray; argues ceilings; insists on per-session consent; reads the room for Cael; accepts the wall; runs witnesses without coaching; plays Coss; writes the page | 15 |
| Quenna | To be right early, and for the bet to hold | Converts the petition; drafts protectively; refuses to lie on the stand; asks the question she cannot file; withdraws her chair for the second sitting; tells him he'll outgrow them | 10 |
| Coss | To do the job honestly and as gently as it allows | Requests the assignment; files the challenge; refuses to requisition Prynn's lending ledger; answers Ilsev truthfully; files a report he knows will be ignored | 10 |
| Edran | For the board to mean something | Files the petition; asks for the fourth exchange; withdraws in public; gives Cael the grid number; doesn't ask; testifies to fact; rebuilds; asks for a rematch | 8 |
| Prynn | A reader worthy of the shelves; her records whole | Opens the code case; forgets to lock; contests the seizure and loses; demands an inventory line per box; holds an empty seat at the hearing; leaves the gap | 8 |
| Naveth | Shelter the mispriced without lying | Slow-walks the Compact; puts the withdrawal option on the table and refuses it; testifies "I'd sign it again" and loses his composure doing it (Book 4 Ch2); re-papers the enrollment | 6 |
| Wray | To be tested | Teaches Brom; promises to rewrite a lecture if proved wrong; names the wall; builds the gauntlet; corrects Cael's pivot | 4 |
| Havel | To be someone who noticed, honestly | Watches Ilsev read the designation three times; writes the third entry; keeps silent | 3 |

Minor recurring (flavor, not counted): the observer child (pending name), the broad defensive student (pending name), the second re-certification candidate (pending name), Ilsev, Yorlan.

### 5.5 New episodes (all fit canon; none alters an end state)

1. **The observer child** (M1, M4, M6, M8). A thirteen-year-old from a guild member family, enrolled under the very provision Cael's enrollment borrows — the category's true user. Sits in the taxonomy lecture. Their taxonomy chart is copied from the classification schedule; the child asks what "shattered" is and why it's not on the chart; the instructor says it's a registry matter, not a Path (M1 plant: the seam is visible in the first third, unrecognized). The child's Kindling arrives at fourteen in week 17 at the town's certification station; Cael waits outside, remembering Denvash; the result is ordinary and the child leaves the observer category. The child is in the gallery for the ruling. Cael's concession protects the provision for children like this one — he never attacks it.
2. **The broad defensive student** (M1, M5, M6, M8): the "furniture" partner at D1, Brom's drill partner, Karis's lattice-study sparring partner in M5, a gauntlet runner in M6. Cael promised himself at D1 to learn his name and thank him; he does both before the term ends.
3. **The second re-certification candidate** (M1, M4): on Lira's shared roster. Her formal reassessment goes badly — no floor rescue, the procedure working exactly as written — and Lira decides on her own to help. Her outcome is the author's; it must not affect Book 4.
4. **Karis vs Edran standings bout** (M2) — see 5.1.
5. **Karis shares one declaration** (M2): part of "Symmetry." Declarations are private and sharing one is intimate (Bible). It is the first standard declaration this book shows, in the LOCKED format, so the reader can set a fragment notice beside a real declaration. Its text becomes canon once drafted — log it in the ledger.
6. **D4 on the page** (M5) and **D6 under Coss's observation** (M6) and **D7** (M8) — see 5.1. D6 produces the single thin exhibit.
7. **Brom's gauntlet** (M6).
8. **Karis's independent read of the schedule** (M7) — the second source of the absence.
9. **Karis reads the waystation's founding entry in the archive's own ledger** (M7): the waystation's founding stands about four hundred years back by that ledger (Book 7 Ch15 relies on this). She reads it as a date, nothing more.
10. **The night between the sittings** (M8): carries Quenna's procedural note the source misplaced into the eve.
11. **Karis asks to read the Power Log** (M8, after the ruling): she promised in M2 never to ask again; Lira releases her; she asks; Cael says yes. This makes Book 4 Ch1's arrangement and the closing line ("three people who know all of it") true. Owner may veto; if vetoed, leave it off-page for the final term.
12. **Letters:** Cael's first letter to Hesk from Greyvane (kept promise, M1) and one brief reply (M3 or M4, author's wording, no new history). The letter to Vell stays the first letter since leaving (M8).

### 5.6 Humor and warmth (author owns the jokes; these are the comic engines)

Lira's rumor market and stolen bread; Brom's "honest food" barley defense and his once-a-week toll-paying; Karis's methodological formality (fraternizing with the opposition on match morning is "methodologically unsound"; she catches the thrown roll); Prynn's dry tea service and her forgetting to lock; Naveth's lawful slowness as an art form; Brom-as-Coss being unnervingly good; the observer child's blunt questions. Let the people who are hurting stop performing at least once each.

## 6. Movements

| # | Chapters | Words | Weeks | Source chapters | Reason in one line |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1–8 | 37,000 | 0–2 | 1, 2, 3, 4 | Make the reader want to be at Greyvane while the system is taught hard and the observer cage becomes a vantage point. |
| 2 | 9–16 | 39,000 | 2–6 | 5, 6, 7 (+ new standings bout) | The first person to ask instead of decide; the Compact's file becomes a field commission's assignment. |
| 3 | 17–23 | 35,000 | 6–8 | 8, 9 | The observer fights in public, and then learns the thing in him might have a handle. |
| 4 | 24–31 | 38,000 | 8–11 | 10, 11 | Records that lie by omission, walls that are honest, and twenty-two nulls that teach a rule. |
| 5 | 32–39 | 41,000 | 12–17 | 12, 13, 14 | The directed acquisition — the book's central fight — and what it costs. |
| 6 | 40–46 | 37,000 | 17–20 | 15, 16, 17 (+ new D6, gauntlet, Kindling) | The machine sends its best hand politely; the boy turns its own code into an opponent. |
| 7 | 47–53 | 35,000 | 21 | 18, 19, 20 | Deliberately the quietest movement: a documents war, the older word found and kept, the eve. |
| 8 | 54–61 | 38,000 | 22–24 | 21, 22, 23, 24 | Two sittings, a crack on the record, the price of daylight, and the road ahead. |
| | **61** | **300,000** | | | |

Average chapter ~4,900 words. The author owns chapter boundaries inside each range.

Why M7 is the quiet movement: its pressure is procedural and archival by canon (deposition, seizure, discovery, rehearsal). Physical anchors are the deposition-as-duel, Prynn's clause fight, Lira's maintenance and dusk spars. Making it fight-heavy would counterfeit the hearing's whole argument — that this opponent is made of paper.

## 7. POV plan

Cael, close third, ~87% (~261,000 words). Cutaways ~13% (~38,000) as sections of 1,500–4,500 words inside Cael-dominant chapters, never alternating chapters. No other POV.

| Cutaway | Budget | Where | What the cutaway may know | What it may never know or say |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Karis | ~10,000 (3.3%) | M2 opening (~4,000: Ternhall, the rumor, the transfer, her week as an ordinary student); M5 (~3,000: the two days writing the paragraph); M7 (~3,000: the founding-era shelves and the founding entry, before the find) | Her own research; the outside shape of his capabilities; her hypothesis; what the terms let her see; after M5, that the fragment came from her | The Power Log's contents (until M8, and never in her POV); any mechanism beyond "observation plus genuine stakes"; any meaning for UNBOUND; any maker. She never says her Ternhall training partner's name (Book 5 Ch12: Ivenne goes unnamed by Karis for two years); the partner may exist as an unnamed absence in a paired-development doctrine |
| Coss | ~10,000 (3.3%) | M2 (~4,000: the hit, the request, the day's approval, packing, the oldest page); M6 (~2,500: the regional office and the packet, the decision to file in person); M8 (~3,500: writing the final report). The faceless-office coda (~500) is impersonal, no named viewpoint | His file, his grade (field agent), his unanswered upward note about a designation he lacks clearance to read; that someone above him was waiting | The Level 4's origin; the directed mechanism (he never learns it); anything about falsification, Arbiters or a maker. He cannot name or describe whoever opens the higher file |
| Lira | ~9,000 (3.0%) | M3 (~2,000: the exhibition from the rail, reading one exchange ahead); M4 (~4,500: the Fenmark file and the reassessment); M8 (~2,500: the first sitting from the gallery and the recess — "not being allowed to hit anyone") | Everything she knows in canon — fragments, the log, her own history | Nothing Cael hasn't told her; no reserved truth |
| Havel | ~8,500 (2.8%) | M7 (~5,000: arrival, recording the deposition, watching Ilsev read the designation three times, the third entry); M8 (~3,500: the recording desk during the long reading of the schedule) | His two prior entries; what he sees Ilsev see; procedure | The designation's origin; any Book 6 truth; nothing about Cael's mechanism |

Cael is the camera for the directed reach, the ruling, the older word, and every reserved-truth boundary. The ruling's last images (hands flat on the wood) stay Cael's.

## 8. Progression plan

### 8.1 Book lexicon (for the ~58 per 10k progression-vocabulary target)

Count: fragment, notice, acquisition, acquired, integration, partial, -adjacent (as in Wind-adjacent), deployment, deployment range, tier, rank, Copper/Iron/Bronze/Silver/Gold as tiers, Iron-equivalent, declaration, Arbiter, Path ladder, standings, advancement, assessment rating, apprenticeship of a fragment, cost/toll when it prices a fragment.

Do NOT count the hearing's legal register (classification, schedule, enumeration, enforcement, jurisdiction). The final third will be dense with it; counting it would invert the formula's front-loaded shape. **Measurement conflict to report, not hide:** the formula's own count method is a word list and would treat "classification" ambiguously. Report both counts at each movement review.

### 8.2 Density by third (formula §4: front-loaded ~1.7x)

| Third (by words) | Chapters (approx.) | Target hits/10k | Approx. hits |
|---|---|---|---|
| First (0–100k) | 1–21 | ~83 | ~830 |
| Middle (100–200k) | 22–41 | ~44 | ~445 |
| Last (200–300k) | 42–61 | ~46 | ~460 |

Per movement: M1 ~90, M2 ~85, M3 ~65 (front half high), M4 ~42, M5 ~45 (a spike at the notice, then quiet), M6 ~40, M7 ~35, M8 ~55 (the closing inventory and the Ember apprenticeship).

### 8.3 Capability table

| Capability | Appears / changes | Where | Cost or limit (must be comprehensible) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wind-adjacent evasion framework | Panel-speed control (M1); anticipation scales with the opponent's body and thins against speed (D1 answer); exhibition (M3); spent hard in the match (M5); two half-second absences ~18 hours after the directed acquisition, clean after three days (M5); pivot a tenth late (D7) | throughout | Fatigue; a choice of direction before execution (the seam the lattice sits on); post-acquisition instability |
| Iron-adjacent surface read | Eyes-closed weight calls (D1); cloth grades (M3–M4); a density before her points bloom (M4 plant); points "as pressure before they were light" (M5) | M1, M3–M5 | Range is contact to a few paces; speed thins it |
| Pressure-adjacent redirect | Banked from every record for the whole book; private drills only | M1, M4 | Shoulder price; three clean uses known (B2) |
| Compression-adjacent absorption | Quarter push (M1) → half (M2) → full slow push and a first fast catch (M4) → one channel crossing (M5) | M1–M5 | Shoulder/sternum toll; mis-routes into a joint when mistimed; the crossing burns the left forearm (healing through M6–M7) |
| Ember-adjacent ignition point | ACQUIRED M5 (week 16), directed, notice verbatim; sits warm; untested until three clean days; first deliberate tests (M6) at contact on a post with Karis observing; shown once at D6 (the exhibit); Karis's first two rewordings (it is not fire) | M5, M6, M8 | Contact-adjacent, single channel, one point; expensive in a way watchers never see; the cost must be shown to the reader the first time |
| Directed acquisition (the Book 3 reveal) | Hypothesis (M3); nulls teach "observation necessary, not sufficient" (M4); stakes complete the circuit (M5) | M3–M5 | Destabilizes the existing fragments for about a day; whether he can refuse it under stakes stays unanswered |
| Tide anomaly (session nine) | Told to Karis; marked "real, unexplained, keep"; reviewed at the inventory | M4, M8 | LOCKED: never reproduced, explained, felt again, or counted |

First-encounter rule (seat): the first deliberate Ember test gets intention, action, manifestation, consequence, cost and Karis's specific reaction. Later uses compress.

### 8.4 Development cadence (formula §5)

Cael: ~4.5 growth/realization beats per 10k (~135 total), even across thirds with a slight middle bump (~38 / ~51 / ~46) — roughly two per chapter, never saved for the climax. Supporting cast as in §5.4 (each 3–18 beats; total spread across ten people).

### 8.5 Rhythm targets (formula §2–3, §8, §9 — apply while writing, measure after each movement)

Sentence mean 14.6, median 11, about 28% of sentences five words or fewer, about 3% forty or more. Paragraph median ~18 words (ASR proxy, directional). Scene breaks marked `---` about every 950 words (~5 per chapter; ~8.7 per 10k). Flesch Reading Ease ~72, Flesch-Kincaid grade ~7. Dialogue tags ~41 per 10k; combat vocabulary ~22 per 10k present outside fights too (the hearing uses it as metaphor honestly). Keep "that" under ~92 per 10k. The current edition measured sentence mean ~17.5 and paragraph median ~44: the new prose must move sharply toward short beats. Measure with `python3 editions/monroe-1.3/tools/formula_metrics.py`.
